Nvidia’s tutorial on genuine good governance, needed by tech bros and governistas alike

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld is the Lester Crown Professor in Management Practice and Senior Associate Dean at Yale School of Management. Steven Tian is the director of research at the Yale Chief Executive Leadership Institute. Stephen Henriques is Senior Research Fellow at the Yale Chief Executive Leadership Institute and former McKinsey consultant.

Charles de Gaulle said, “The graveyards are filled with indispensable men.” That rings especially true despite the pathetic cries of the ‘Bring Jensen Back’ bros.

Nvidia’s strong earnings release today proves once and for all that Nvidia does not need Jensen Huang to return to flourish as a strong American national champion. Interim CEO Colette Kress deftly raised forecasts moving forward, pointing to the underlying strength of the business, which is far greater than any one individual, while winning plaudits for running the earnings call with command, presence, and impressive candor. And indeed, new board chair Harvey Jones definitely closed the door on Jensen Huang’s return as CEO amidst news that the SEC is now probing Huang and Nvidia.

All this makes the unresolved governance questions and lessons from the Nvidia debacle even more important.

In the aftermath of Jensen Huang’s ouster from Nvidia this week, a tiny band of tech bros, perhaps intoxicated by their own sudden wealth and prominence, have cast good governance principles to the wind by unrealistically advocating for the return of Jensen Huang, decrying the “woke” politically correct management policies which they believe led to Huang’s exit. At the same time, a chorus of finger-pointing good governance advocates has joined the fray, throwing every pet theory of organizational structure and regulatory restraints on risk-seeking entrepreneurs. Both camps ignore the real collapse of board oversight, which is the tragically poor judgement of the CEO matched with the faulty character and faulty process of board oversight and not its clunky bureaucratic mechanics.

The tech bro cheerleaders rooted for a messianic return of Huang, despite the ongoing investigations into his wide array of apparently corrupt actions and character collapses, such as his blatant violations of Nvidia’s code of conduct and unresolved national security concerns. The arrogant, naïve fantasies in the chants of these tech bros is encapsulated by Telegram founder Pavel Durov’s X post declaring, “Jensen made a rookie mistake. He had a board”, apparently forgetting that any executive who is entrusted with other people’s money owes a fiduciary duty to their shareholders first, above their own self-enrichment. They are happy to snag the money but then to dodge accountability.

Then, on the other end of the critics’ spectrum, good governance advocates, self-declared governistas, have rushed to offer a smattering of bad solutions for Nvidia’s governance woes. These proposed solutions are as ludicrous as they are trite, and as irrelevant as they are ineffectual.

For example, Charlie Elson at the University of Delaware somehow baselessly managed to blame a favorite bogeyman of many governistas, dual-class shares, for Nvidia’s woes — even though Nvidia has a single-class stock structure and not dual-class shares at all, a rather inconvenient fact which nevertheless did not detract from Elson launching into his tiring tirades. Elson bewilderingly argued in Directors & Boards that “in the end, this may spark, and I hope it does, a real reconsideration of the whole notion of dual-class stock in publicly traded companies”, as if that would be a panacea for all of Nvidia’s board woes, an argument which The New York Times also haplessly repeated.

Similarly, governance professor Richard LeBlanc of York University proposed an independent chair as the panacea to all Nvidia’s governance woes, even though Nvidia already had an independent lead director who was the former CEO of Cooley Law Firm, Stephen Neal.

All of these proposed governista solutions are irrelevant cliches offering insufficient and misguided recipes.

Between clumsy New York Times op-eds, delusional Silicon Valley social media campaigns, and even an ideologically charged, anti-management Delaware Chancery Court judge, it appears that both anti-oversight tech bros and scolding governistas need to relearn some basic lessons about corporate board diligence.

Here’s what these self-anointed governance experts miss and what the real lessons for boards from the Nvidia crisis are:

All of the misguided proposed solutions from governistas tend to draw from the same wrongheaded playbook that ISS and Glass Lewis have preached for decades, recited on cue by the governistas: split the chair and CEO roles, encourage more so-called independent board members, tally the committees, and count board diversity. As we have argued in Fortune, this tired and trite playbook has never been able to tell a good board from a bad one.

Consider how Nvidia was an ISS and Glass Lewis box-checker’s showpiece. Every director but Huang was technically independent. It had an independent lead director who was a former CEO of a respected law firm; independent audit, compensation and governance committees and committee chairs; annual elections for every board position without any staggered terms; and the ‘one vote per share’ principle so beloved by governistas. Huang was not even chairman of his own board: Nvidia had no chairman at all until he left, so Nvidia even fit the technical separation of roles box with a separate lead independent director. In June, shareholders re-elected all ten directors, the CEO with 99% of the vote, with ISS supporting the entire director slate this year, as it has each year for many years now.

But the Nvidia crisis reminds us all just how misleading are the metrics of ISS and Glass Lewis’ checklist. This ‘check-the-box’ approach does absolutely nothing to actually weed out genuinely responsible boards from boards asleep at the switch operating on a bedraggled autopilot. Indeed, it is ironic that many of the worst corporate governance crises this century have been at companies which were darlings of the governista community.

For example, Enron had 12 independent directors among its 14 directors, split the chairman and CEO roles in its final year, and had a former dean of Stanford’s business school, an accounting professor, chairing its audit committee, winning widespread praise from governance advocates with one governance publication even naming the Enron board one of America’s five best boards in 2000 – right before Enron blew up, exposed as a fraud. Similarly, Silicon Valley Bank had an independent chair and a board that was entirely independent other than its CEO, and 10 of Lehman Brothers’ 11 directors were independent.

Indeed, when Stanford researchers tested ISS’s then-flagship governance rating against five measures of how companies actually fared, including restatements, shareholder lawsuits and stock performance, they found no significant correlation with any of them.

The first author wrote in Harvard Business Review in 2002, “In the wake of the meltdowns of such once great companies as Adelphia, Enron, Tyco, and WorldCom, enormous attention has been focused on the companies’ boards … And yet a close examination of those boards reveals no broad pattern of incompetence or corruption. In fact, the boards followed most of the accepted standards for board operations: Members showed up for meetings; they had lots of personal money invested in the company; audit committees, compensation committees, and codes of ethics were in place; the boards weren’t too small, too big, too old, or too young.”

Alas, the reliance upon such banal bromides of supposed good governance, lead to the recitation of platitudes in publications but worse yet miss the targeted need entirely – much the way the Goodhart’s Law of economics warns us to not confuse the metrics of the tool with the assessment of the underlying phenomena itself. This is also parallel to intelligence testing, where the test is an indicator of performance against the unidimensional artificial metrics of the intelligence test as an instrument itself and misses the many other untested dimensions of intelligence as Harvard psychologist Howard Gardner has pointed out in his classic Frames of Mind analysis.

Another favorite bogeyman of the governance community tends to be dual-class shares, and this long-held bias is once again rearing its ugly head with governance commentators rushing to blame dual-class shares for the Nvidia crisis – even though, ironically and inconveniently, Nvidia only has one class of stock, and Nvidia has never actually had dual-class shares at all.

Indeed, ISS now generally universally recommends against directors at companies with unequal voting rights, without exception, reflecting how widely held this erroneous strain of thinking is entrenched in some circles of the governance community.

By way of full disclosure, we are sometimes wrongly identified as an ‘advocate for dual-class structures’, in the words of Vice Chancellor Travis Laster of the Delaware Chancery Court, who has been overturned by the Delaware Supreme Court no less than 35 times in his idiosyncratic and tortured readings of the law.

But we are not the universal dual-class share advocate Laster misleadingly portrays us as. Our view is much more nuanced and captured in the conclusion of my Harvard Law Corporate Governance Forum piece: “In evaluating the outperformance of dual-class shares and in evaluating common scenarios where dual-class shares work especially well, balanced against common pitfalls, the evidence is clear that for companies, there is no single right answer between dual-class, multi-class, or single-class shares. It is all situational, dependent on a company’s circumstances, the demonstrated caliber of their leadership, and the environment in which they operate. Just as dual-class shares are not the right structure for all companies; dual-class shares can make sense for certain companies, in certain scenarios. These crucial nuances are often lost in what has increasingly become the single-minded zealotry of ‘one share, one vote’ advocates, seeking to impose single class structures on all companies irrespective of circumstance.”

Indeed, what critics of dual-class shares like Laster and Elson miss is that some of the worst corporate collapses and frauds of the modern era had ‘one share, one vote’ share structures, ranging from Enron, Worldcom, Tyco, Arthur Andersen, Bear Stearns, Lehman Brothers, Wirecard, FTX, Silicon Valley Bank, and First Republic Bank, all of which share a single commonality: they did not have dual-class shares. Thus, critics like Charlie Elson who blame dual-class shares for Nvidia’s woes are committing the sin of governance by non sequitur. It is like prosecuting a car company for making potential getaway cars for a robbery which was actually committed on foot. Clearly, a formula that indiscriminately, automatically scores Berkshire Hathaway’s share structure as a vice and Nvidia’s as a virtue, without human discernment or space for an iota of discretion, is measuring based on the wrong yardstick.

What failed at Nvidia was not any of the factors identified above; rather, it was the caliber of the directors and the culture amongst the board that proved problematic.

As the first author wrote in the seminal Harvard Business Review article in 2002, ‘What Makes Great Boards Great’, great boards are social systems before they are governance structures. They build enough trust that management brings bad news early, with open and candid discussions and no secrets kept. Great boards treat dissent as a duty, and promote cultures where questioning authority is not only welcome but expected. They rotate roles so that no director is typecast as a permanent cheerleader or scold.

As my former colleague, the former Yale School of Management Dean and former SEC Chairman Bill Donaldson said at a Yale CEO Summit many years ago, “On the subject of board governance… corporate governance has become a cottage industry with lots of opportunists, and there’s some very superficial thought out there about what a board really is…..The least examined is that it is a social entity. It has human beings on it. Nobody talks much about that. Everybody says, ‘Oh, you ought to have certain people on the audit committee because they’ll be independent, etc., etc.’

“There hasn’t been nearly enough work done on the social contract on the board and the recognition of what is really going on, on that board. And a lot of times, what’s really going on bears the seeds of horror stories. Perhaps there’s a group that’s aligned improperly, and that group dominates either through tenure on the board or whatever and allows bad things to go on because nobody really recognizes what those interpersonal relationships are.”

Donaldson emphasized that great boards hold each director accountable and grade their own performance as hard as the CEO’s, and this gets to the caliber of the board as well as the culture of the board. None of this works without directors of the stature to look a monarch in the eye. As I wrote in The Hero’s Farewell, monarchs are larger than life CEOs who would leave office only by death or palace revolt. With a visionary founder, a board’s job is to retain and contain: keep the genius, check the hubris.

But Nvidia’s board lacked the stature to contain or check Huang’s impulses; they could only retain and worship. Evaluating the board members individually, no Nvidia director has ever run an S&P 500 company and none has national security experience, at a company whose chips are a national security asset. Industry peers Microsoft, IBM, and Dell each have at least four current and former big-company CEOs on their board; Nvidia had none. And apparently, the board was asleep at the switch on succession planning, which is one of the primary functions of a board. Nobody thought to groom even an emergency successor for a CEO with more than 60 direct reports and no president or chief operating officer beneath him, and a system of doing business through handshake deals to centralize all power in himself. And when the crisis came, the board’s first instinct was to vouch for him before its own investigation had begun before finally surrendering to the facts of the situation.

Similarly, it was plainly inadequate and insufficient for Nvidia’s board, to simply defer to the professional judgment of their advisors, such as their external accountant PwC or their external law firm Cooley. PwC signed off on financial reports with what many experts consider to be questionable accounting, obfuscating the geographic origins of Nvidia’s revenues and the end users of sensitive Nvidia chips in ways that could possibly open the board up to legal claims of failure of oversight if not even perhaps fraud in the eyes of many experts.

Even today, there still seems to be a latent expectation amongst some in tech circles, including apparently from Huang himself, that a weak Nvidia board will just collapse and bring Huang back in deference to the founder if enough pressure is brought to bear. Huang himself put out an unusual statement this morning, saying that “If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO”, even though Huang is now the target of no less than 4 ongoing government investigations across congressional oversight hearings and federal probes as well as multiple shareholder lawsuits. Certainly, Marshal Philippe Petain of Vichy France; the deposed Emperor Napoleon, Enron’s disgraced Jeff Skilling, Worldcom’s deposed Bernie Ebbers, and other deposed monarchs dragged out kicking and screaming would have likewise enthusiastically volunteered to return after being thrown out by their constituents. Bewilderingly, even some proxy ratings firms like Egan Jones seem to be lending implicit support to the ‘Bring Jensen Back’ movement, declaring that “It is not entirely clear the board acted in the best interest of shareholders” by removing Huang, looking past the evasion of sanctions, national security, and accounting integrity concerns to say the board should not have removed Jensen on the basis of shareholder returns from prior, possibly misleading financial reports.

What Nvidia’s board woes highlight is that contrary to the outdated ideas of some – for example, how Travis Laster would define ‘good faith’ as checking hot tempers at the door – the measure of genuine board diligence is the willingness to foster board cultures of open dissent and genuine partnership with CEOs over blind deference.

The latter challenge is especially salient when it comes to working with larger-than-life founders. The right answer is not for boards to go to war with these creative geniuses by trying to suppress them, or to lose trust by trying to fire them over foot faults. Rather, it is up to the board to develop governance practices that show they know how to partner with the genius they are privileged to have among them.

The boards of Microsoft, Amazon, Dell, Starbucks and, ultimately, Apple figured out how to work with Bill Gates, Jeff Bezos, Michael Dell, Howard Schultz and Steve Jobs, forging alignment between the needs of their founder visionary CEOs and the shareholders. These boards helped their founders appreciate and address their human frailties, whether with non-accusatory accountability (as with Gates through Microsoft anti-trust litigation) or during health crises (Jobs’ battle with cancer and need for credible succession plan).

It is not easy to regulate genius and creative founders do not resemble rational people as they challenge the daunting odds of success that most people wisely avoid. They are also often obsessed and single minded about their visions – which seem to many to be delusions seeking a mirage of unattainable entrepreneurial success. Yet their innovations can disrupt and transform society on the few occasions where they succeed.

Sigmund Freud reminded us that society is changed by its discontents. Painter Robert Motherwell confessed that as an artist there is never satisfaction, because creative joy is mixed with the anguish of what could be. But at the same time, boards must recognize that they are not dealing with an artist painting in a private studio on their own canvas, but rather an artist who is using other people’s resources, painting on a public canvas, and so must listen to other voices.

Only by forging genuine partnerships with their CEO built on trust and mutual candor, can boards strike the right balance between blind deference and overbearing suffocation, particularly when it comes to working with bigger-than-life visionary founders. The lesson for boards, and what both sides of the critics’ spectrum misses, is that the solution is not related to the structure of the board; rather, it is the professional courage to do their jobs fully, and nobody can legislate performance through checklists or rulebooks.

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld is the Lester Crown Professor in Management Practice and Senior Associate Dean at Yale School of Management.

Steven Tian is the director of research at the Yale Chief Executive Leadership Institute.

Bachelor's Peter Weber Reveals He Married Pregnant Jadeyn Carr

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bachelor Nation alum Peter Weber and Jadeyn Carr privately tied the knot Nov. 11 while expecting their first child after hard-launching their relationship in August.

Peter Weber got his wish of finding a co-pilot for life.

The Bachelor star shared that he and Jadeyn Carr said "I do" in a private ceremony on Nov. 11, following the couple announcing their first pregnancy in September.

As the commercial flyer, nicknamed Pilot Pete, told fans aboard the Bachelor Nation cruise Nov. 17, "I can officially say that on 11/11, I married the love of my life."

The special gathering was made even more magical thanks to its unique wedding date, known for being a prime day to manifest big dreams coming true.

That said, keeping the conclusion of his journey to finding his final rose—which began on Hannah Brown’s season of The Bachelorette in 2019—under wraps was ultimately too exciting to withhold from his loyal fans any longer, as he revealed during a panel on the cruise.

"I was debating of revealing this here, but you guys have all been here, following my journey for a very long time," the Traitors alum explained. "I appreciate that, all the support."

While the 35-year-old, who hard-launched his relationship with the model in August, hasn't yet shared any further details about their nuptials, he did gush about the impending marriage in a cryptic post on their wedding day, when he shared an all-smiles selfie featuring Carr and her engagement ring with the caption, "Marry Me."

Following the affair, Golden Bachelor alum Renee Halverson-Wright gave some insight into the sweet occasion.

"It was the most beautiful, touching wedding ceremony," the 70-year-old replied to Bachelor Nation's Instagram, adding the celebration was "filled with so much love and exactly the way they both wanted it!"

And while the former reality star—who led season 24 of the ABC series in 2020—has kept much of their relationship private, his Aug. 13 carousel debuting their romance showed that his loved ones were already fans of his future bride. Case in point? Weber’s brother Jack Weber commented on his post, writing, "Love this," while his mom Barbara Weber wrote, "Happiness is…" alongside red heart emojis.

Following his time on The Bachelor, Weber dated Flanagan, who appeared on his season of the dating reality series. The two reunited in April 2020, but called it quits that following January. While he and Flanagan rekindled their romance in September 2022, he revealed they were no longer together in May 2023.

Will you accept this invitation to check in on the Bachelor's most memorable relationships? If so, read on...

Lest the couple forget where their journey began, they need only walk past the master bedroom in their Dallas-area pad. That's where the graphic designer has framed the final final rose that season 17's Bachelor presented her back in that Thai forest in late 2012, right after he promised to tell her he loved her every day of the rest of their lives.

"It made sense to dry this one and make it permanent because it was accompanied by a ring and a proposal, so this is a really special thing," Catherine explained to Entertainment Tonight in 2017. Because that last rose ceremony, complete with an elephant ride and a $75,000 Neil Lane diamond, is as much part of their story as the the sweet, suburban life that followed with kids Samuel, 5, Isaiah, 3, and Mia, 2. "We don't want to shy away from the fact that we got engaged and fell in love on The Bachelor," she said, "so that is the only red rose I will allow in my house!"

The OG Bachelorette, Trista was the first to prove that a stint in the mansion could lead to love, marriage and even Bachelor babies. "Nowadays they shoot the show for, I believe, three months. So it's like 12 weeks," the author shared with Us Weekly of her 2003 season. "My show was shot in six weeks. So Ryan and I met, got engaged, got married a year later, after knowing each other for six weeks. So that's how it used to be."Their decade years holding it down as the show's success story have taught the Colorado-based pair they can handle pretty much anything. Including months sheltering in place with their kids, son Maxwell, 14, and daughter Blakesley, 13. "It's really hard for us to get a date night. I think when the kids are working, we try to take advantage of that time as much as possible," she said of scheduling around the firefighter's early bird tendencies. "But yeah, we're good. We're 16 years and this too shall pass, as they say."

Who even remembers that the sweet divorced dad originally proposed to Melissa Rycroft? Okay, probably a lot of people, as his 2009 After the Final Rose switcheroo still ranks as one of the series' most shocking moments. But if all's well that end's well, the only thing that matters is that the Seattle-based parents to daughter Riley marked 10 years of marriage this past February. "Let me apologize publicly again for putting you through the Bachelor mess that I created," Jason, also dad to teen Ty, wrote in an anniversary tribute. "With that being said... it built an incredible, unique, foundation for us to build our family—showing that we can make it through anything."

In true Bachelor fashion, native Tennesseean Peyton simply knew when she laid eyes on her future husband. Though it wasn't season 10 lead Andy Baldwin. Nearly four years after the naval officer sent her home, the Bachelor Pad alum turned up at another series-related event in 2010 and spied Chris, fresh off his showing as Ali Fedotowsky's runner-up. "It's one of those things where I've had a ton of serious relationships," she later explained to People, "and I met this kid and he was genuine, outgoing and hilarious and we just click."By the following June, the landscaper was proposing at his home in Cape Cod. And more than a decade, two kids (daughter Lyla, 5, and son Hayes, 3) and a few HGTV and DIY Network shows later, they credit the series with laying their foundation. As Peyton put it in an August 2020 Instagram post, "Without the show I'm not sure our paths would have ever crossed."

For as much as the wedding dress designer seemed to key in on Brooks Forester during her 2013 season, it was sweet mortgage lender Chris and his thoughtful prose that proved to be the perfect fit. More than seven years after their L.A. vows (during which the Desiree Hartsock Bridal designer wore two of her own original creations) they're settled in Portland, Oregon, with sons Asher, 5, and Zander, 3. "If our life were a storybook I would be the damsel in distress," the host of podcast Heart of Purpose surmised in a February 2020 post. "Chris would be the funny yet charming knight who saves the day, Asher would be the pragmatic problem solver while his little brother would be the carefree sidekick trying to kiss all the ladies!" And they would all enjoy a happy ending. As Desiree put it in a post acknowledging the choice of Clare Crawley as Bachelorette, "Reality TV worked for me!"

The gold standard in Paradise success stories, the married duo have inspired countless rose seekers to turn up in Mexico with visions of Neil Lane diamonds and Chris Harrison-officiated vows dancing in their heads. The couple's time on the spin-off's second season yielded both, and with son Reed's November 2020 arrival, they'll tie the Lowes for most Bachelor offspring. "Life gets crazy sometimes," Jade wrote in a January anniversary post of juggling 4-year-old daughter Emmy and 2-year-old son Brooks, "but I wouldn't want to go through this hand-in-hand with anyone but you. I would choose you 1000 times over."

When the Bachelor in Paradise alum returned to the beaches of Nayarit, he was initially hesitant to pursue the Bachelor finalist, worried his peripatetic lifestyle wouldn't "mesh" with the former beauty queen's. But nearly three years (plus one brief breakup and a scary December 2019 ski accident that saw Dean laid up in Switzerland for weeks) later, they, and the van he calls home, are jelling just fine. "Obviously I was wrong about that because things are going well," he shared on his podcast Help! I Suck at Dating in October 2019, months before they started wearing rings to show their shared commitment. Mostly, he appreciates that she has a healthy level of snark and the ability to handle his jokes. "I can be myself around her," he continued. "Which is a nice change."

Though he skipped the Neil Lane-sponsored proposal on Bachelor in Paradise in 2018, Kevin was ready to take a knee by the following fall, proposing in his native Toronto where he and Astrid share a home. While they've since had to push off their planned November 2020 vows in her Florida hometown, "We're actually okay with it," Astrid wrote on Instagram, noting that making the decision to postpone due to the coronavirus pandemic allowed them to focus on their connection more than the myriad wedding details. "I wouldn't want to tackle 2020 & the rest of life with anyone else by my side." Now "back in the full swing of wedding planning," as she put it on Instagram, they'll wed this October just weeks before son August's first birthday.

Even the most wishful of Bachelorette fans likely weren't betting on JoJo and Jordan's journey leading them to an altar. Because with the persistent rumors of the former Vanderbilt quarterback's ill intentions ramping up even before they took the stage for the requisite grin-and-gush After the Final Rose appearance in 2016, it seemed unlikely she would still be wearing her 3.75-carat Neil Lane diamond years later.Which, she's not. But only because Jordan presented the real estate developer with an upgrade in August 2019. Though their summer 2020 wedding plans were scrapped due to the pandemic ("I know it will be all more worth the wait," JoJo wrote in a post acknowledging their would-be June day), they tied the knot in May 2022. "You make me laugh, love me endlessly, encourage me to be brave, and always, without fail, are my biggest cheerleader," the native Texan wrote in a post on their fourth anniversary. "Can't wait for what the rest of our lives have in store for us."

Consider Ashley the poster child for ignoring all of the standard he's-just-not-that-into-you signs. Because after four years of lusting for the guy she declared her dream man upon sight, she was proven right at their August 2019 vows in Newport, R.I. Every bit as all-in as his bride, who welcomed their son Dawson in January, Jared reflected on their perfect day on Instagram, writing, "Ashley is my best friend, my person, my soulmate and now my wife. I love this woman more than words will ever allow me to express."

Who needs Neil Lane and a beach backdrop when you have this much imagination? The Dallas-based real estate agent went big when proposing to his Paradise love in May 2019. "She thought it was a normal shoot day," Adam told E! News of his plan to turn a set-up for her online boutique into an impromptu engagement session. "We were at the helicopter pad on the roof and Raven had roses in her hand. She was throwing the petals up for cute photos. She did a few practice runs and then the videographer slipped me the ring and once she threw the petals up, I got down on one knee. I blacked out, but I got everything out that I wanted."

‘If you don’t act now, it will be existential’: Defense tech CEOs say Pentagon’s ‘Industrial Age’ bureaucracy must adapt

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As AI-enabled drones transform the way wars are fought in Ukraine and Iran, two leaders of military tech startups warned on Tuesday that the Pentagon has to move faster to keep up with modern warfare.

“There’s a growing realization that if you don’t act now, it will be existential in the near future,” Tyler Sweatt, CEO of defense software deployment company Second Front Systems, told Fortune’s Allie Garfinkle at the Fortune 500 Innovation Forum.

While venture money is pouring into defense tech at record levels, the Pentagon hasn’t sped up to match, and investors want returns. Sweatt said that pressure means defense tech companies will likely consolidate in “a tranche of deals” in the next six months. Shield AI, the autonomous weapon developer whose CEO Gary Steele joined Sweatt onstage, has already started: It announced Nov. 5 that it’s raising $2.5 billion to support its acquisition of radar systems company Echodyne in a transaction valued at $5.3 billion.

Sweatt and Steele said the Pentagon’s lengthy approval processes and outdated systems could keep new tech from reaching troops quickly enough. Sweatt described the Pentagon as an “Industrial Age organization” bogged down with “big long bureaucracies” and “antiquated technology production lines” that will have to change.

“We’re at the beginning of this journey, not the end of this journey,” Steele added. “It will take time because things move slowly; the technology is not moving slowly.”

Sweatt and Steele’s concerns echo those of a Government Accountability Office (GAO) report published in July, which found the average time needed to deliver a capability across the Pentagon’s major weapon acquisition programs rose to over 12 years, and the watchdog predicted this average “will likely increase in the future.”

Steele contrasted those delays with Shield’s autonomous fighter jet, which the company designed to fly alongside aircraft piloted by humans using its Hivemind software. He said his company expects the time “from conception to first flight” will be less than two years. By comparison, Lockheed Martin’s F-35 started development in 2001 and flew in 2011, and in total took 23 years from being awarded the Pentagon contract to being cleared for full-rate production.

But Steele said Shield can develop its aircraft faster because it being autonomous means it’s not endangering pilots in the testing process. In separate tests last month, Shield’s Hivemind software executed directions from a F-22 pilot and enabled the coordination of multiple uncrewed aircrafts called CCAs.

“We can go faster because we’re not putting people’s lives at risk every single day,” Steele said. “We can think about that iterative development cycle to get very different outcomes at speeds that never could have happened before.”

Venture capitalists are now keen on investing, though that hasn’t always been the case. They deployed a record $37.9 billion into defense tech across 269 deals in Q3 2026, up 117.5% quarter over quarter, according to Pitchbook data.

“Defense tech was in the same category as porn and gambling, and that’s all changed,” Steele said.

But Sweatt, whose company is valued at $1.2 billion per Pitchbook data, added that the influx of funding and increased defense spending by U.S. allies doesn’t mean new tech will reach the military immediately.

“It takes a minute for that tail to really reach the tooth,” he said, referring to the lag between investment in defense tech and its actual implementation in the battlefield.

That delay could also test whether defense tech companies can justify their valuations, with Shield doubling its valuation to $28.8 billion from $12.7 billion in March. Anduril CEO Brian Schimpf, whose company is valued at $61 billion, even said at the Fortune Brainstorm Tech conference in June that defense tech could be in “a bit of a bubble.”

“I think the next 24 months are probably going to confirm or deny the bubble hypothesis,” Sweatt said.

Jeff Bezos has some advice for Nvidia’s board on its CEO search

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 17, 2026 at 5:05 pmNovember 17, 2026 at 5:07 pm

Jeff Bezos says Nvidia should look inside the company for its next CEO, and he doesn’t give an outsider much chance of success. The Amazon founder, now co-CEO of AI engineering startup Prometheus, made the comments Tuesday at Semafor’s Silicon Valley & The World event.

“If you don’t bring Jensen back, I would not hire an outsider. Nvidia is too complicated,” Bezos said. Outside CEOs at complex companies, he added, “have a vanishingly small chance of succeeding,” and he would consider hiring one “a last resort.”

Nvidia’s board says it has no plans to bring Huang back. Harvey Jones, the longtime director named the company’s first board chair last week, opened Nvidia’s earnings call Tuesday afternoon by saying the board “does not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO.”

Jones said the search will include internal and external candidates, and that the board wants a permanent CEO in place quickly but won’t rush the pick.

Huang, the Nvidia co-founder and CEO for 33 years, had posted a statement on X that morning offering to return. He said he had “made serious mistakes” in his personal life and would cooperate with the board’s independent investigation.

“If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO,” he wrote.

Nvidia announced Huang’s resignation last Thursday, a day after the New York Times reported that he’d had an affair with an Nvidia executive who reported to him. Days earlier, he admitted to an affair with a woman who U.S. officials believe was recruited by Chinese intelligence, according to the Wall Street Journal.

CFO Colette Kress, who spent 13 years at Microsoft earlier in her career, is serving as interim CEO.

Two major Nvidia investors, Altimeter Capital’s Brad Gerstner and Atreides Management’s Gavin Baker, asked the board to leave open “the potential return of Jensen Huang.” Analyst Dan Ives of Yorkville Ives wrote in a note Sunday that talk of a Huang comeback is “ridiculous.”

The Amazon precedent (not really): Bezos has been through a founder handoff of his own. He turned over the Amazon CEO job in 2021 to Andy Jassy, a longtime insider who built AWS. When Semafor raised the comparison, Bezos called it a poor one, saying there has never been a more carefully planned transition.

He said he didn’t know enough about what happened in Nvidia’s board meetings to say whether Huang should return. But he called Huang a remarkable founder who deserves “admiration and respect no matter what.”

Huang himself said in a Bloomberg interview recorded Oct. 14 that he hoped his successor was already inside the company.

The Nvidia comments came in a wide-ranging interview. Bezos also said Blue Origin is using Prometheus tools on two projects, called President Trump by far the most communicative of the presidents he has worked with, and said Robert Pattinson was the clear choice to play the next James Bond after screen tests of five finalists.

Business as usual, officially: Nvidia reported third-quarter revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year earlier, including $107.6 billion from its data center business. On the call, it raised its forecast for next fiscal year’s revenue growth to about 75%, from 70%.

Kress didn’t mention Huang by name on the call. Asked about the leadership transition, she said customers, suppliers and employees remain behind the company, calling it “business as usual.” She ended the call with a short pep talk that finished, “We’ve got this.”

In case the board was wondering about one outsider in particular, Bezos is spoken for. During the Semafor interview, he described running Prometheus as “Nirvana.”

SoFi Is Targeting 100 Million Products by 2030. Here’s What $10,000 Invested Today Could Be Worth if It Gets There.

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mentioned on the latest earnings call, management’s explicit goal gives investors plenty to cheer about.

Investors are learning firsthand how a company's stock price can deviate from its underlying fundamentals. In 2026, shares of SoFi Technologies (SOFI -3.84%) have tanked 40% (as of Nov. 12). That's a disappointing performance that showcases just how pessimistic market sentiment has become.

However, the business is doing quite well. Through the first nine months this year, its adjusted net revenue soared 42% year over year to $3.7 billion. This proves that the long-term growth case still holds, which is why investors are drawn to the fintech stock in the first place. It looks like there is a sizable runway for expansion ahead.

This is exactly what the management thinks. CEO Anthony Noto is targeting 100 million products by 2030. That would be roughly 4 times the 26.7 million reported as of Sept. 30. This figure measures the number of distinct offerings that have been adopted. It's a key performance indicator for the leadership team.

Here's what a $10,000 investment in SoFi shares could be worth if the company achieves its goal.

In 15 of the last 16 quarters, SoFi reported earnings per share that exceeded Wall Street analyst estimates. Observers would take this as a clear sign that executives operate with conservative assumptions. It's about managing the investment community's expectations.

It seems this playbook was recently thrown out the window. Noto's optimism was striking. During the Q3 2026 earnings call, he said, "We see a clear path to achieving 100 million products by the end of 2030, which is a 4x increase from where we stand today."

The growth formula to get there is straightforward. SoFi's user base, now at 17.1 million, will expand to 50 million, translating to a 193% increase. Given that memberships jumped 35% year over year last quarter, shareholders might find this target to be reasonable. This business has had zero issues bringing on new users.

To get to 100 million products, though, the company will without a doubt need to drive higher engagement. Right now, it averages 1.56 products per customer. Basic arithmetic tells us that each of the 50 million potential users will need to sign up for two products by 2030 to hit the 100 million mark. And SoFi would have to generate the same amount of revenue per product that it does today.

Noto also mentioned that quadrupling the products would essentially quadruple the earnings base. Any investor would be elated if a company they owned posted 300% profit growth in four years. If SoFi's valuation, currently at a forward price-to-earnings ratio of 18.1, doesn't change, then a $10,000 starting investment would grow to $40,000 by the end of 2030 based strictly on the bottom line's trajectory.

SoFi is one of the most exciting stories in the financial services sector. Its fundamental performance, which has been outstanding, gives management the credibility to reveal targets that might seem implausible. But there are no guarantees.

Noto calls out artificial intelligence and blockchain technology as two super cycles that are ushering in a new era of growth for the industry and this business. These perceived tailwinds surely play a role in SoFi's long-term target, as they enable it to introduce new innovations to better serve customers.

What if these capabilities don't pan out as management hopes? That could slow its impressive growth.

There's always a chance that each member doesn't use more products or that the revenue per customer doesn't rise as anticipated. And the stock's valuation multiple could compress. These variables are hard to predict.

Even Noto ended the earnings call by saying that this is a "very ambitious goal." He also added that it's "achievable." SoFi is aiming high. Investors have to respect that at least.

AMBCrypto reported recently that the Bitcoin [BTC] price move down to $62k acted as a leverage flush. It remains to be seen whether the bulls can enforce a recovery from here.

In a post on X, Glassnode showed that the price was trading just above a dense cluster of short-term holder supply. The darker cells denote more BTC supply in STH custody at that cost basis.

Deeply negative apparent demand (30-day sum), with a 30-day reading of -247,700 BTC and a yearly measurement of -1.21 million BTC, highlighted severe market stress.

Crypto analyst Darkfost wrote that the metric, which is an indirect on-chain proxy based on new issuance versus changes in supply inactive for over a year, does not measure net cash outflows.

Nevertheless, it has historically been a reliable indicator that helps confirm trend strength. Right now, any price rebound without apparent demand easing toward neutral or positive levels would signal weakness.

Flowtica Launches Verso, an E-Paper AI Recorder for Meeting Notes

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Flowtica launches Verso, a magnetic AI recorder with an e-paper display for live transcripts, meeting summaries and follow-up tasks.

Flowtica has launched Verso, a magnetic AI voice recorder that puts meeting transcripts, summaries and follow-up tasks on an always-on e-paper touchscreen. Announced on November 17 after its earlier unveiling at IFA 2026, the device is designed to attach to a smartphone while giving users a separate place to check information without opening an app.

The launch combines familiar AI note-taking functions with a different hardware interface. Instead of making the recording itself the entire product, Flowtica is positioning Verso as a display for the work that surrounds a conversation: preparation before a meeting, important moments during it and outstanding actions afterwards. The distinction is practical. A summary stored in an app still needs to be opened; an e-paper screen can leave the next task in view.

Verso uses a 259-pixel-per-inch e-paper touchscreen inside an aluminum-magnesium alloy body weighing approximately 70 grams, according to the company. It supports MagSafe-compatible mounting on iPhones and attachment to Android phones using a magnetic ring. Flowtica also describes desk-stand, lanyard and magnetic-surface configurations, allowing the same device to move between a phone, a workspace and a shared planning surface.

The display is intended to show live transcription, structured summaries and AI-generated answers. E-paper can retain an image without continuously refreshing the screen, which suits the product’s emphasis on keeping information visible rather than adding another stream of notifications. That does not make the recorder or its AI processing power-free, and the company’s battery claims should not be confused with independently tested recording endurance.

A dedicated Ask AI button lets users query previously captured notes, request context before a meeting or draft a follow-up email. FlowMark provides a way to flag an important moment while a conversation is taking place, giving users an explicit signal to return to rather than relying solely on an automatically generated summary.

Flowtica says Verso combines four MEMS microphones with a contact microphone. The company claims pickup from as far as five meters away and support for distinguishing up to 15 speakers. Those are manufacturer specifications, not results from independent testing, and actual performance will depend on the room, background noise and how participants speak.

The contact microphone supports phone-call capture when Verso is attached to a smartphone. Online meetings are covered through Flowtica’s Mac desktop application, while the device itself records in-person conversations. Together, these routes are intended to keep different kinds of meeting material within one note-taking workflow.

The launch announcement describes multilingual transcription, speaker separation and structured summaries as cloud-based AI functions. Buyers therefore should not assume that the presence of onboard storage means transcription and AI queries run entirely on the device. Flowtica lists 64GB of storage, Bluetooth and Wi-Fi connectivity, USB-C charging and battery life of up to 72 hours. The device also includes an NFC digital business-card feature for sharing contact details.

During recording, Verso displays an indicator that Flowtica says cannot be disabled. That makes capture more visible to people nearby, but an indicator alone does not settle an organization’s rules about recording conversations or sending meeting content to a cloud service. Teams considering the device will still need to assess how it fits their own confidentiality and data-handling requirements.

The same distinction applies to generated output. A transcript or summary can help participants retrieve a discussion, but users should review names, numbers, commitments and assigned tasks before treating them as an authoritative record. Verso’s interface may make those outputs easier to consult; it does not remove the need to check them.

Verso is available through Flowtica’s website, with paid Premium and Unlimited subscription options. The company expects Amazon availability in early 2027. The launch is a bet that AI meeting tools can become more useful by changing where their output appears: not just in another software dashboard, but on a small, persistent display that stays beside the conversation.

Hackers Are Using AI to Speed Through Cyberattacks, FBI Warns

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The agency says Artex is helping attackers target organizations worldwide, including banks, government agencies and healthcare providers.

An open-source AI tool designed to find security flaws is helping hackers launch cyberattacks. The FBI says hackers are using Artex, an AI-powered security testing tool, to break into organizations worldwide. In an advisory on Friday, the agency said some attacks took less than 3 hours from selecting a target to gaining high-level access to its systems. Attackers have used Artex since at least August against banks, government agencies, technology companies and healthcare providers.

The advisory was issued the same day communications problems prompted a nationwide Federal Aviation Administration ground stop and a day after a major Microsoft 365 outage began. Microsoft attributed its disruption to technical problems, and the FAA says it has found no indication of a cyberattack.

An FBI official told ABC News⁠ that Artex doesn’t appear to be connected to either incident or to other recent disruptions, including banking outages involving JPMorgan Chase.

The warning adds to concerns about the security risks posed by AI agents, which can carry out tasks with little human supervision.

Artex automates security tests that organizations use to uncover weaknesses. According to Microsoft’s analysis, its agents can examine defenses, exploit flaws and extract data. Attackers can use that automation to try several ways into a network simultaneously.

The FBI cited attacks against South Korean financial institutions that exposed the personal information of approximately 67,000 people. Microsoft warns that attackers breached over 2,250 organizations’ Microsoft 365 systems in a separate campaign, using stolen ConnectWise backup credentials alongside the Artex tool.

Because customers had already authorized the backup software, attackers could use its access without logging in as an individual user. Microsoft cautioned that a successful sign-in doesn’t necessarily mean data was accessed or stolen. The automated attacks could trigger security alerts, but their speed left defenders little time to respond.

This FBI Cyber advisory on ARTEX was released last Friday, 11/13. It is not a new announcement, and the FBI has not issued any additional warning since. The guidance in the advisory remains current.We released this advisory last week because as we head to the end of the year,…

Amazon Web Services’ Nov. 13 research described another scheme: Stealing credentials for AI services, and then using that access to hunt for more credentials at someone else’s expense. Amazon Threat Intelligence uncovered the activity through decoy systems posing as AI services. It linked the campaigns to Artex with moderate confidence but couldn’t independently verify results the agents reported on systems outside its monitoring.

Amazon said the attacks relied on default passwords, exposed login details and outdated software, all of which hackers can find and exploit faster with AI.

Artex’s developer removed the original GitHub repository in October after reports of abuse, but copies remain available, the FBI said. The agency recommends limiting access to sensitive systems, reviewing software permissions, replacing exposed credentials, and monitoring unusual account activity or large data transfers.

Lautaro Martinez torna ad allenarsi in gruppo: il messaggio social del capitano dell'Inter

Lautaro Martinez torna ad allenarsi in gruppo: il messaggio social del capitano dell'Inter

Gemini 4 Hydrogen: a faster model for general purpose robotics

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Over the past month, Gemini 4 Argon and Gemini 4 Carbon brought frontier physical reasoning to robotics: understanding 3D scenes, reconstructing real environments in simulation, practicing tasks in sim before running them on hardware, and directly controlling robot arms from camera images. Last week, we introduced Gemini 4 Hydrogen, which brings strong spatial and embodied reasoning to a much faster model built for speed and efficiency.

To help developers put Hydrogen to work, today we're launching the Robotics plugin on Google Antigravity: a set of skills for hardware onboarding, simulation and robot control turning Antigravity into a one-stop robotics workbench. Together, they enable a developer to go from a robot in a box to one that can perform a working application in an afternoon. To install the plugin, open Antigravity, go to Customizations and search for Robotics on the Build With Google shelf.

Gemini 4 Argon and Carbon showed that a general model can drive a robot arm directly, and Gemini 4 Hydrogen preserves that ability. Give it a goal in plain language and a camera view, and it plans the task, writes and runs the control code, and keeps adjusting until the job is done. On the Artificial Analysis Robotics 0.1 benchmark, Gemini 4 Hydrogen controls a real 6-axis arm through eight everyday tabletop tasks, from pouring a drink to packing blocks into a box. Hydrogen scores 58, similar to Argon at a lower cost per token.

Hydrogen brings the specialized skills of our Gemini Robotics ER 2 (Embodied Reasoning) models into the core Gemini 4 model series, and it beats them on nearly every axis. On the public ERQA benchmark, Hydrogen outperforms Gemini Robotics ER 2 on spatial reasoning tasks such as multi-view correspondence and 3D trajectory reasoning. For developers using Gemini Robotics ER 2 today, we recommend trying out Gemini 4 Hydrogen for these workloads.

A fast, capable model is only half the story. Building a robot application has always meant gluing together drivers, calibration scripts, simulators, perception code, and a UI, which requires robotics expertise and a lot of time. The new Robotics plugin for Google Antigravity packages that work into skills Gemini can use on your behalf:

Here's what that looks like end to end: set up a robot and build real applications.

Starting from an empty desk, a developer plugs an I2RT YAM arm and an external depth camera into a laptop and asks Hydrogen in Antigravity to bring it up. The plugin's Skills install the software, find the camera, calibrate the arm, and run test motions. The arm is calibrated and ready for tasks in about 10 minutes. A similar flow also brings up a low-cost SO-ARM101 arm and an industrial grade FR3 Duo from scratch to live waving.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Once the robot is live, you can use a simple prompt to create a basic task within just a few minutes:

Once the robot is live, the next step is building a simulation you can trust. With the Robotics plugin, MuJoCo runs directly inside Antigravity with dedicated creation, auditing, and rendering skills. This allows you to build and preview high-quality simulated scenes and assets—whether described, imported from links/documents, or uploaded via workspace photos—right alongside your code. You can then "vibe sim" by adjusting shapes, positions, and physical properties through chat to verify reaching and grasping before deploying to physical hardware.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The workbench with Hydrogen and Robotics plugins lets you go from an idea to a repeatable working application in real world in a much shorter time, by iterating and validating it in simulation.

A developer starts with a whiteboard sketch: a conveyor belt carrying mixed snacks, with a robot arm picking and sorting them into bins. From one prompt of the task with images of a real scene, Hydrogen constructs an interactive MuJoCo simulation of the cell, testing and validating the grasping policy in sim across multiple runs. The developer then deploys the verified policy directly to the robot in the real world. When nuances emerge (such as tuning gripper closure force or refining sorting rules), the developer iterates in natural language, asking Hydrogen to adjust the controller to iterate. Within about an hour end-to-end, the arm reliably sorts snacks off the moving belt.

Hydrogen's efficiency makes it practical to run a multi-step robot application all day. In this example, a developer uses Hydrogen to build a small web storefront for a soap shop, then connects it to two YAM Pro arms. When an order comes in, the arms set out a packing bin, pick the ordered mint and citrus bars, and pull a printed thank-you note from a printer.

With just a few prompts, the FR3 Duo simulates a chess board and then can play chess against itself or take on human opponents.

Gemini 4 Argon pushes the ceiling of physical reasoning. Gemini 4 Hydrogen makes those capabilities fast and affordable enough to use every day, and the Antigravity Robotics plugin puts them in a workbench where anyone can go from a robot in a box to a working application. We can't wait to see what you build.

Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Android’s source code is big – the Android 17 open source release has 1,084 Git repositories, 1.62 million files, and takes 300 GB for a built workspace. Additionally, in a typical Android source tree with hardware vendor code and first-party applications, the size increases by 2-3x. Traditionally, it took over 30 minutes to fetch Android Open Source Project (AOSP) source code and well over an hour to build on even highly capable developer workstations. As software development evolved over the last 2 years, modern coding harnesses and LLMs have accelerated the development of many projects. These tools are used today for Android operating system development, but the long sync and build times make them less efficient. Both human developers and agents need quick setup, parallel workspaces, and fast build-and-test iteration times.

We built Another Build File Service (ABFS) to address these bottlenecks. ABFS is a full suite of software across a Linux kernel filesystem module, user-space daemons, and server-side infrastructure. ABFS uses content-addressability across the entire stack to lazy-load and cache source code, developer workspaces, intermediate build files, and build results.

Today, we are excited to announce that ABFS is open source on GitHub.The impact of ABFS to Android is:

ABFS already powers parallel workflows for developers and agents in Android, but it is designed to scale to other massive Git-based projects.

In the Android build system, developers define build rules in Blueprint files (Android.bp), Soong compiles them into Ninja files, and Ninja executes the final build graph (see the Android build overview). Similar to the Make build tool, Ninja is timestamp-based. If a build step’s input file is newer than the existing output file, Ninja rebuilds the output file.

Analyzing the AOSP code for Android 17 shows the sheer disk footprint and file count involved in a single checkout and build.

In a typical Android tree that includes hardware-specific repositories, board support packages, first-party applications, form-factor-specific code, etc., the code size is 2–3 times the size of AOSP (over 3,000 Git repositories), compounded across multiple active SKU and legacy maintenance branches.

Building the Cuttlefish emulator target executes a build graph with 192,249 Ninja actions. While local incremental builds are fast once a tree is already built, every new workspace, branch switch, or ephemeral agent session starts cold.Even though typical developer changes touch only 1-10 different Git projects, someone building locally without any remote execution might waste up to 80 minutes on a 64-core machine repeating work their CI system already built. Even if a developer edits a core library like system/libbase, 97% of the build graph is identical to the CI build.

Sharing cached build outputs across CI and local machines is also tricky in a codebase of Android's age and rate of change. Build rules occasionally introduce hidden dependencies (such as passing an undeclared schema file inside raw compiler flags), over-declared inputs, or undeclared outputs. In timestamp-based builds, hidden dependencies silently produce stale incremental artifacts, forcing developers to wipe output directories and run clean builds.

ABFS makes building Android fast by replacing massive local source checkouts with a virtual, content-addressable filesystem (CASFS). ABFS streams files on demand only when a build actually touches them and utilizes shared build caching to help eliminate redundant downloads and compilation.

On the server side, Git-pushers continuously ingest new Git objects from Gerrit or other Git hosts into a horizontally scalable gRPC ABFS Content-Addressable Storage (CAS) Server that indexes Git content, workspace snapshots, and build step results. On the developer host, a local Least Recently Used (LRU) Cacheman daemon, Mount Daemon, and Tap utility connect user-space build tools to the kernel filesystem so checkouts and builds stream only what they need. See the full ABFS architecture on GitHub.

At the core of the client is a virtual filesystem called Content-Addressable Storage Filesystem (CASFS), a Linux kernel module that exposes Git SHA content hashes via extended attributes. When a file's hash is set, CASFS populates its metadata immediately and downloads file bytes via Cacheman only when needed:

Initial checkouts download only lightweight directory metadata prepopulated with Git hashes. CASFS uses Git's object model natively, allowing standard git and repo operations to work transparently.

CASFS can also tap build processes. Tapping records every file actually read, written, or deleted into lists called patches, which are stored alongside cached build outputs. Ninja-go replaces timestamp checks with two-phase content-addressable caching:

Ninja-go accelerates Ninja-based builds like Android and Chrome; other build systems can integrate with ABFS's caching APIs using the same model (see the two-phase caching walkthrough on GitHub).

Each mounted checkout attaches to a persistent workspace that eagerly syncs modified files, git state, and repo state through Cacheman to the ABFS CAS Server—keeping mounts synchronized across sessions or across multiple developer hosts.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The benchmarks below show the mean time of 3 Android runs on a GCE n2d-standard-64 VM with 64 vCPUs and 256 GB of RAM. The ABFS remote server was running in the same cloud network as the client.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Because ABFS does lazy loading and local cache deduplication, developers and their agents can now mount multiple concurrent Android workspaces on a single machine, benefit from a shared local build cache, and achieve massive scale in development time.

An agent can check out a full Android tree in under 10 seconds and perform a full build in under 10 minutes. Agent swarms are empowered to iterate, review, and test in parallel, which can lead to higher quality solutions and speed.In the demo below, an agent makes a system/libbase fix on the latest AOSP release, then 4 parallel agents simultaneously backport, build, and test the fix across 4 more AOSP releases on a single VM.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

We released ABFS as open source because we want developers, Android partners, and any large-scale Git-based project to benefit from what we have learned and be able to scale.

While Android’s Soong/Ninja pipeline was the first acceleration target, ABFS was built to be extensible to other build systems. Content-addressable build systems like Bazel map naturally onto CASFS and there is already a proof-of-concept integration with Bazel. Chromium, the Android Common Kernel, and other Google projects are exploring ABFS compatibility.

Explore ABFS, CASFS, and Ninja-go on GitHub, and reach out to us at abfs@google.com.

For help with the Google Cloud server-side, infrastructure-as-code, and CASFS for Cloud Workstations and Kubernetes, please reach out to abfs-gcp@google.com.

We’d like to extend a special thanks to our significant contributors for their work on this project:

Haamed Gheibi, Erik Sanders, Rob Seymour, Taylor Santiago, Andrew Hsieh, Eric Taseski, Kiyoung Kim, Hung-Wei Chen, Jose “Pepe” Galmes, Ronald Li, Yu-Che Wang, Roger Ellis, Sandeep Patil, Amith Dsouza and the entire Android Build team

How You Can Use an Upper-Lower Workout Split for More Gains

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you want to build strength and muscle, split your workout plan in two.

THE STRUCTURE OF your workout over the course of weeks and months should depend on more than just the latest fitness fad. When you’re working toward a goal—whether that’s tied to building muscle, forging strength, losing weight, or improving performance—you’ll have the most success if you follow a plan. That plan (often called a workout split) should take into account arange of factors, including your specific goals, your experience level, schedule, and available equipment.

And one of the most well-known (and easy-to-understand) approaches to this is the Upper-Lower split, which is one of several workout splits organized around training different parts of the body on different days.

An Upper-Lower Split program is broken down exactly as its name implies. One day you’ll train the muscle groups in your upper body (think chest, shoulders, back, arms); then the next you’ll train your lower body muscles (quads, glutes, hamstrings, calves). In general, that means your upper body gets to recover on days you’re training your lower body, and vice versa.

This approach is one of a handful of training splits that works very well if you’re trying to add muscle, or if you’re somebody who’s chasing body recomposition. If you’re trying to build muscle, the more frequently you can train a muscle group with effective, hard sets, the better.

And an upper-lower split lets you train each muscle group at least twice a week, with solid rest in between each session. You’re training each muscle group more frequently than you would if you were doing a bro split (training just one body part a day). And you’re able to do more sets and exercises for key muscle groups than you would if you were doing a full-body training split.

Let’s break down the details of the upper-lower of split, along with a sample session you can use to put it into practice.

Sets and reps: 3 sets of 8 to 10 steps per leg

Sets and reps: 3 sets of 8 to 10 reps per leg

IF YOU WANT to understand the logic behind the workout above, here’s what you need to know. You'll alternate focusing on the upper body muscles and the lower body muscles in this split. Since you're no longer training the entire body, you'll have the time and energy to include a few more accessory motions. Over the course of the week, aim to hit all of the following movement patterns.

There are multiple ways to build an upper-lower split depending on your goals. But in general, think about including three to five exercises in every session.

YOU’RE ABLE TO hit major muscle groups more completely by splitting the body in half. Since we're able to add in a few accessory motions, we can start training in several different planes of motion, too. This split is well suited for recovery, since your upper body rests while you're working the lower body, and vice versa. That allows you to increase your frequency of training.

YOU’RE STILL WORKING quite a few major muscle groups in a single workout, which can be fatiguing for the accessory movements added onto the end. If you’re in the awkward schedule space between having three and four days you can train, you might struggle to hit the upper/lower/upper/lower cadence that will be most effective—so stick to the more flexible full-body split.

Brett Williams, NASM-CPT, PES, a senior editor at Men's Health, is a certified trainer and former pro football player and tech reporter. You can find his work elsewhere at Mashable, Thrillist, and other outlets.

Reading car fault codes from a web page: Web Serial and Web Bluetooth vs. cheap ELM327 clones

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A cheap ELM327 adapter, Chrome, and two browser APIs are enough to read engine fault codes without installing anything. The protocol part is small; most of the code deals with how real adapters and real browsers misbehave, and that part is what this post covers.

Disclosure: I built diagnost.net, and everything below comes from its client-side code (elm.js, obd.js, kline.js). It is served to every visitor, so you can check any snippet against the live page at https://diagnost.net/connect. The code comments are in Russian; I paraphrase them here.

Platforms: Windows, macOS, Linux, Android. Safari and Firefox don't expose these APIs. On iPhone, no browser can reach either Bluetooth or USB, because the restriction is in iOS itself, so Chrome on iOS is in the same position as Safari.

The page has two transports: elm.js talks to an ELM327 over USB or BLE, and kline.js talks to the car directly over K-line through a dumb USB cable (more on that later). Both expose the same function:

Frames come back already stripped of headers and checksums. The OBD-II layer (obd.js) — reading stored codes (mode 03), pending codes (07), freeze frame (02), sensors (01), VIN (09), clearing codes (04) — is written once and doesn't know what sits under it. If you build anything on top of serial hardware, this split is worth copying: all of the hardware-specific pain stays below one interface.

navigator.serial.requestPort() only opens if it's called during a user activation. That sounds trivial until you do async work first. The page probes the adapter at several baud rates, which can take a while, and if it then decides to show the picker, the gesture has expired and you get a SecurityError.

First, when the user clicks "pick another adapter", the picker is requested synchronously, before awaiting anything. Second, if the page discovers late that it needs a picker, it doesn't try to open one; it shows a button, and that new click provides a fresh gesture.

For repeat visits the page calls navigator.serial.getPorts() and opens previously granted ports without asking again. If that list is empty, getPorts() returns quickly enough that requestPort() is still inside the same gesture.

Also note: in Firefox and other browsers with a stubbed implementation, navigator.serial may exist while the picker throws NotSupportedError. Feature-detecting the object isn't enough; handle that error as "not supported".

This one costs time if you don't know it. port.open({ baudRate }) resolves at any speed, because the USB-serial driver doesn't check it. ELM327 clones differ: most cheap ones run at 38400, firmware 1.5+ and OBDLink at 115200, very old ones at 9600. The only reliable test is to send ATZ and wait for the > prompt.

heard tracks whether anything at all arrived from the port. "Silence" and "something answered, but not an ELM" lead to different messages for the user.

Reading is a plain pump loop on port.readable.getReader(), decoding with TextDecoder and feeding a buffer. One detail matters: everything in the buffer before a new command starts is discarded. Otherwise a late tail from the previous attempt (for example, garbage from the wrong baud rate) gets attributed to the next command, and every response after that is off by one.

If a write is still pending, the writer stays locked, releaseLock() doesn't free the stream, port.close() rejects, and the next connection attempt hits the same port as "busy". The close sequence that works:

Every step has a time cap, because waiting for a flush that never comes is how tabs hang. A failure of port.close() itself is not hidden: the promise rejects, since the port may still be open. The page also releases the port on pagehide.

The DOMException name is the main signal you get, and some names are ambiguous:

The last row matters for UX. Telling the user "close the other program" on NetworkError would be wrong whenever the cable had simply been pulled, so the page only says "busy" when that's proven (InvalidStateError) and shows a neutral "couldn't open the port" otherwise.

Every ELM327 BLE clone hides its serial channel behind a vendor service. Web Bluetooth only lets you access services you listed up front, so the list doubles as optionalServices — without it getPrimaryServices() returns nothing:

After connecting, the page walks the services (known ELM services first, so it doesn't latch onto some unrelated pair) and picks the first characteristic with notify/indicate plus the first with write/writeWithoutResponse. It writes with writeValueWithResponse if the characteristic supports write, otherwise writeValueWithoutResponse.

If the adapter is paired with the operating system, the browser won't see it in the picker. Users have to remove the pairing first.

Some cheap BLE clones advertise a service that never returns its characteristics (on KONNWEI adapters it's 180A, Device Information). Chrome walks every service on the device, gets stuck on the broken one, and the getPrimaryServices() promise never settles — no resolve, no reject. Without a guard the page showed "Connecting to the car…" forever.

A BtStage error gets its own message: the adapter responded but didn't give the browser its service list, so try USB or another adapter.

Once a stage times out, the underlying gatt.connect() may still resolve later. And for the same adapter, the browser hands back the same BluetoothDevice and the same GATT server object. If the user retried in the meantime, a late disconnect() from the old attempt would kill the new session. The page stores the owner of each GATT server in a WeakMap keyed by device.gatt, and only the latest attempt is allowed to disconnect.

The opposite also needs handling: on every failure after connect(), the page has to call gatt.disconnect() explicitly. Otherwise the tab keeps holding the adapter, and the next attempt, from this page or from another program, can't get it.

ELM327 speaks text: send 0100\r, receive lines, and a > means "ready for the next command". Sounds like a simple request/response queue. On BLE it isn't, because the notification with the answer can arrive before the writeValue promise resolves. The first version treated > as "done" and sent the next command while the previous write was still in flight. Chrome replied with GATT operation already in progress.

The fix is to queue whole transactions and treat a command as finished only when both events have happened:

The same queue serves both Web Serial (writer.write) and Web Bluetooth (writeValue*), so USB gets the same guarantee for free.

The true passed to fin() on timeout or write failure is the other half. After a timeout you don't know whether the command reached the adapter or whether its answer is still coming; on BLE the write may still be sitting in the stack. If you send the next command, it may receive the previous answer. So the link is marked failed: the active command and the whole queue are rejected with LinkFailed, new send() calls reject immediately, and nothing more is written. Only a new connection recovers. The UI catches LinkFailed, stops live polling, and says the connection was lost. I prefer that to "wait for silence and continue", which gives you data shifted by one response.

Timeouts: 8 s per command, 12 s for long OBD requests, and 30 s for the first 0100, because after ATSP0 the adapter cycles through protocols and that can take up to about 20 s. If the AT commands passed but 0100 timed out, the adapter is alive and the car is silent (ignition off, usually), and the user is told exactly that.

The response parser splits into lines first, drops status words (SEARCHING..., NO DATA, ERROR, ...), and only then converts hex. On K-line each ECU answers on its own line; on CAN a long response arrives as 0:, 1:, ... chunks preceded by a length line. An early version glued lines together, and on a real BMW E46 the genuine codes P0313, P0171 and P0174 came out as P1301, C3101 and C3443. Wrong parsing here doesn't crash; it produces believable, non-existent fault codes.

Each code is two bytes: the top two bits pick the letter (P, C, B, U), the rest are digits. On CAN there's also a count byte right after the service ID, so the protocol number from ATDPN decides the offset. The page then fetches short descriptions for the found codes and links each one to its own page, like https://diagnost.net/codes/P0420.

One more rule: if the car answered nothing at all, the code list is "not read", not "no faults". A green "all clear" on a silent ECU would be false.

Some users plug in a BMW K+DCAN cable instead of an ELM327. It's a transparent USB↔K-line adapter: no AT commands, and since K-line is half-duplex, the cable echoes back every byte you send. ATZ comes back as ATZ at every baud rate. That echo is how the page recognizes the cable.

Web Serial turns out to be enough to drive K-line directly: it supports 10400 baud, and port.setSignals({ break: true }) pulls the line low. ISO 9141-2 starts with the ECU address 0x33 sent at 5 bits per second — 200 ms per bit — using break as the zero level:

setTimeout drifts by milliseconds, so the code sleeps until 8 ms before the mark and spins the rest. Per the measurement in the code comments, setSignals costs about 0.3 ms and the spin keeps precision under 1 ms.

Two browser-level surprises from this path:

ISO 9141-2 via 5-baud init was verified on a real E46. The KWP2000 fast-init path exists but hasn't been tested on a car yet. All of this only applies to K-line cars, roughly pre-2007; D-CAN over a browser serial port isn't possible.

Web Serial and Web Bluetooth are usable for real hardware today, in Chromium browsers. Budget most of your time for the edges: which errors are ambiguous, which promises never settle, and which events arrive out of order. One design decision I would repeat: making error types specific — "adapter silent", "car silent", "not an ELM", "port held by this page" — because each one tells the user to do something different.
